{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Di9fNdr5gh6VMC4RKWCitP","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/results.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"6f4f2c20-a339-4f13-8f71-f1f2ee4ee159","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:11:22.891Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/results.tex","oldString":"confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47","newString":"confirms bounded association (interaction = 0.47","originalFile":"\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nUnless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}\n\\label{sec:count_curves}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{\\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves for NER and RE. Error bars show $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\\times$ 25 batches per seed. RE accuracy declines monotonically with count; NER recall exhibits a U-shaped pattern with minimum at intermediate counts. Full-schema points (rightmost, hollow markers) represent the complete type inventory with no batch sampling variance. \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty. The pattern replicates across three seeds (42, 123, 456; max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD); intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$ at every count level (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$, pooled across three seeds). Count curves (25 batches per level) establish trend direction with error bars; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0]), roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Dimension~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Dimension~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$), modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r < -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Dimensional Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded association in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality: high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":84,"oldLines":7,"newStart":84,"newLines":7,"lines":[" "," Error taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches."," ","-These results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded association in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_019KmJEK6V3Sqe5fX1UkG6vX","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/main.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"e08ee63d-da2c-4c3f-a1e5-adfd2813e458","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:11:26.760Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/main.tex","oldString":"supporting bounded interaction between count and confusability effects","newString":"supporting bounded association between count and confusability effects","originalFile":"\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}\n\\usepackage{listings}\n\n\\title{Does Type Confusability Shape Schema-Induced Degradation\\\\in Training-Free LLM Information Extraction?}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\input{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\n\\input{related_work}\n\n\\input{experimental_setup}\n\n\\input{results}\n\n\\input{sdgd_negative}\n\n\\input{discussion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B with cross-model validation on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Whether patterns hold for proprietary models (GPT-4, Gemini) remains unverified. Dimension~2 was not robustly detectable on NER at 8B scale, likely due to floor effects.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Modest pairwise effect size.} Pairwise SCS explains 1.3--1.7\\% of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality likely account for the remainder.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Factorial interaction tests.} Within-dataset count $\\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). However, a FewRel factorial with TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded association ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp, interaction = 0.47\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), and cross-pool slope consistency ($p = 0.60$) corroborates stable rather than amplifying sensitivity.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Decomposition and metric scope.} RE batch composition is tested on FewRel only; NER merge alternatives remain unexplored. SCS relies on embedding cosine similarity; label entropy or ontological depth may capture complementary dimensions. For Few-NERD, SCS is computed from the extraction model's own embeddings (Qwen3), creating a shared-representation dependency. MiniLM cross-check yields a conservative but directionally consistent estimate ($r = 0.172$ vs $r = 0.138$; confusion matrix correlation 0.894), suggesting the effect is not an artifact of shared representations.\n\n\\item \\textbf{NER evaluation metric.} Our exact-match NER evaluation conflates boundary errors (partial span overlap) with type errors (correct span, wrong label). Relaxed matching may yield different confusability effect sizes, as boundary ambiguity may correlate with schema complexity independently of type similarity.\n\n\\item \\textbf{RE format specificity.} RE's clean dimension separation may reflect its closed-set classification format. FewRel combines training and validation splits for 80 types and characterizes classification accuracy rather than end-to-end extraction.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Causal mechanism.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway. The controlled stratification provides evidence consistent with a directional relationship but does not establish interventional causality.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Data contamination.} FewRel (2018) may appear in pretraining corpora. However, train-origin relations show numerically lower accuracy than val-origin ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p=0.06$, n.s.), opposite to the contamination hypothesis. Moreover, the monotonic count-dependent degradation curve (85.7\\%$\\to$50.4\\%) cannot be explained by uniform contamination, which would elevate all conditions equally. BCS slope consistency across independent and within-pool conditions (permutation $p = 0.60$) provides a robustness check that is difficult to reconcile with non-uniform contamination, which would differentially affect specific type subsets. DocRED (2019) provides a lower-contamination-risk replication with consistent BCS--accuracy slopes ($R^2 = 0.53$). Adding train-origin ratio as a covariate leaves the BCS partial effect unchanged ($p = 0.245$ for origin; Appendix~\\ref{app:split-origin}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} Following the argument that independent replications do not require family-wise correction \\citep{rothman-1990}, while acknowledging the opposing view \\citep{perneger-1998}, we treat each dataset as a separate test of the same hypothesis. Within-dataset secondary analyses are reported with individual $p$-values; core claims rest on effects reaching $p < 0.001$.\n\n\\input{checklist}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{sec:appendix_figures}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 0.702 & 0.640 \\\\\n64 & 0.273 & 0.304 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDecline & $-$43\\,pp & $-$34\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model count-curve replication on Few-NERD (text-match recall). Both models show a general declining trend in recall with schema size. Per-batch correlation $r = 0.725$. Qwen3-32B values use the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}) for fair cross-model comparison; the count-curve results in \\S\\ref{sec:count_curves} use the v1 prompt, yielding higher absolute recall (0.72 vs.\\ 0.64 at count${}\\,=\\,1$) due to over-prediction under v1.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{crrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Qwen3-32B}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n8 & 78.1\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$27.1 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n16 & 75.5\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$23.9 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy.  High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% regardless of model, while low-BCS accuracy reflects model capacity differences.  The confusability gap ($\\Delta$, in pp) is 77--87\\% as large on Llama-3.1-8B as on Qwen3-32B.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n & & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{BCS Bin}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-7}\n\\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{Error} & \\textbf{Solo} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{Mid} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{V.High} & \\textbf{$\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & IntraSub & 0\\% & 1.6\\% & 10.6\\% & 49.4\\% & --- & 1.000 & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nRE  & IntraSub & 0\\% & 4.2\\% & 13.0\\% & 20.3\\% & 32.3\\% & 0.954 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & Missed & --- & 42.8\\% & 64.8\\% & 49.9\\% & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nRE  & Missed & --- & 10.4\\% & 11.3\\% & 13.1\\% & 15.7\\% & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) and missed-entity rates by BCS bin, with Spearman $\\rho$ computed over $n=154$ individual batches (RE) and $n=148$ batches (NER). NER has 4 BCS bins; RE has 5.}\n\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n\nSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\nper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\subsection{Practical Routing Strategies on RE}\n\\label{sec:appendix_routing}\n\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ Single-Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle (gold label) & 72.4\\% & +22.0\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMax-conf (positive only) & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nNone-aware & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nMargin (positive only) & 35.9\\% & $-$14.5\\,pp \\\\\nMax-conf (all) & 27.7\\% & $-$22.7\\,pp \\\\\nRandom & 14.7\\% & $-$35.7\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (no decomposition) & 50.4\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Routing strategy comparison on FewRel 80-relation RE with graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition. Oracle requires gold labels. All practical (non-oracle) strategies perform below the single-pass baseline. The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\\%.}\n\\label{tab:routing_strategies}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nTo formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded interaction between count and confusability effects.\n\nA separate within-dataset $2 \\times 2$ factorial on FewRel (count $\\in \\{8, 16\\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) did not reach equivalence ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.56$), likely due to limited power from fewer count levels and narrower BCS contrast. The Few-NERD factorial (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}\nWe set $\\pm 3$\\,pp as the smallest interaction that would materially alter practitioner decisions: a 3\\,pp interaction atop the 2.2\\,pp confusability main effect would represent ${>}130\\%$ amplification, fundamentally changing whether confusability warrants mitigation. At $\\pm 2$\\,pp (marginally equivalent, $p = 0.047$), interaction remains bounded below the main effect itself.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{crrl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \\textbf{$t_{\\text{upper}}$} & \\textbf{$p_{\\text{TOST}}$} & \\textbf{Conclusion} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\\\\n$\\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\\,pp, 90\\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\\,pp) is bounded below 2\\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$), confirming that the $\\pm 3$\\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\\pm 1$\\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors, central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality (the count of strong semantic neighbors) becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal, a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\\,=\\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level (high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably) rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability dimension.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary source. The underlying confusability signal (pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship) persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}\n\nTo verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Mean Acc} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nV2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\\\\nV2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\\\\nMinimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\\\\nMinimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r < -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}\n\\label{tab:prompt_variants}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \\approx 0.60$; MAX $p \\approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Schema} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n16-rel & 8 & 88.9\\% & 79.8\\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\\\\n80-rel & 8 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\\\\n80-rel & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}\n\\label{app:within-pool-method}\n\nA potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & $0.027$ & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\nFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & $0.76$ & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p \\approx 0.60$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n\\label{tab:within_pool}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p \\approx 0.60$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).\n\nIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \\approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect; the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied, but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\nMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\nFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}\n\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n\n\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n\nThe following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims; the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n\n\\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}\nWhen the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ [95\\% CI: 0.001--0.151], well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} / (\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\\sim}5\\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \\to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \\emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.\n\n\\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}\n\\label{app:split-origin}\n\nFewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \\emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \\geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\\texttt{train\\_origin\\_ratio} = n_{\\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, reflecting the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:\n\\[\n\\text{accuracy} = \\beta_0 + \\beta_{\\text{BCS}} \\cdot \\text{BCS} + \\beta_{\\text{origin}} \\cdot \\text{train\\_origin\\_ratio} + \\varepsilon\n\\]\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\\beta_{\\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 10^{-16}$ (vs.\\ $\\beta = -0.412$, $p < 10^{-16}$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\\beta_{\\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.\n\n\\section{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}\n\\label{app:variance-decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4 & 0.0889 & 0.0122 & 53.5 \\\\\n8 & 0.0820 & 0.0098 & 70.0 \\\\\n16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\\\\n32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\\\\n40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\\\\n64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\\\\n80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition on FewRel (3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed variance averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists, so batch variance vanishes by construction.}\n\\label{tab:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{table}\n\nIntra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\\times$ across count levels 4--64 (mean 125$\\times$), confirming that random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability. At count 80, the full schema admits only one composition, leaving model inference noise as the sole variance source (seed SD = 0.88\\,pp).\n\n\\section{Cross-Domain Connections}\n\\label{app:cross-domain}\n\nThe schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \\citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-dimensional decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole predictor; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications: a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics, and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.\n\n\\end{document}\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":184,"oldLines":7,"newStart":184,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}"," \\label{sec:appendix_factorial}"," ","-To formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded interaction between count and confusability effects.","+To formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded association between count and confusability effects."," "," A separate within-dataset $2 \\times 2$ factorial on FewRel (count $\\in \\{8, 16\\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) did not reach equivalence ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.56$), likely due to limited power from fewer count levels and narrower BCS contrast. The Few-NERD factorial (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01HfxtNkCtWASfVpmaGamFvt","type":"tool_result","content":"experimental_setup.tex:60:SCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\nexperimental_setup.tex:76:\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\nintroduction.tex:4:Training-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\nrelated_work.tex:10:\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.\nrelated_work.tex:12:\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\nresults.tex:29:We test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\nsdgd_negative.tex:21:Oracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key predictor of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"0d90fb68-fbac-4b85-bd3f-b9e942e535fa","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:11:42.336Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"experimental_setup.tex:60:SCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\nexperimental_setup.tex:76:\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\nintroduction.tex:4:Training-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\nrelated_work.tex:10:\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\nresults.tex:29:We test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key predictor of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}
