{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Kd56HiuJiGJXkdoekhNuvF","type":"tool_result","content":"58\t\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n59\t\\label{sec:scs-validation}\n60\tSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n61\t\n62\t\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n63\t\\label{sec:exp_design}\n64\t\n65\tWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n66\t\n67\t\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\n68\tWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n69\t\n70\t\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\n71\tWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n72\t\n73\t\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n74\t\\label{sec:metrics}\n75\t\n76\t\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n77\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"a88c3028-ec8e-49ea-b655-c7e1fde8dd24","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:11:57.489Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex","content":"\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n","numLines":20,"startLine":58,"totalLines":83}}}
{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-6","id":"msg_01C5kafRbZRbL3fdvFczk8Ku","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01PiWCutqmLGQsZ2zdE1cF1M","name":"Edit","input":{"replace_all":false,"file_path":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex","old_string":"a partial proxy---context, type frequency","new_string":"a partial proxy; context, type frequency"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":949,"cache_read_input_tokens":117420,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":949},"output_tokens":39,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"06746559-4a2e-43d5-badd-90e2b1bc31fe"}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01PiWCutqmLGQsZ2zdE1cF1M","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"53207f29-7992-4415-a0dc-82caf9233664","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:12:00.453Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex","oldString":"a partial proxy---context, type frequency","newString":"a partial proxy; context, type frequency","originalFile":"\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances uniformly across test instances; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 embeddings because its 66 fine-grained types include same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\\ ``person-actor'') that MiniLM's 384-d space does not separate well; a cross-embedding check confirms directional consistency (MiniLM $r = 0.172$, Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}). SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which eliminates model-level randomness; bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":57,"oldLines":7,"newStart":57,"newLines":7,"lines":[" "," \\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}"," \\label{sec:scs-validation}","-SCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.","+SCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy; context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design."," "," \\subsection{Experimental Design}"," \\label{sec:exp_design}"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0161cnDmkvTec54ZT2gfy6pT","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"b74e9c3e-2d3c-4225-baf0-0b837eb30bc1","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:12:03.091Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex","oldString":"text-match F1---a prediction is correct","newString":"text-match F1, where a prediction is correct","originalFile":"\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances uniformly across test instances; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 embeddings because its 66 fine-grained types include same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\\ ``person-actor'') that MiniLM's 384-d space does not separate well; a cross-embedding check confirms directional consistency (MiniLM $r = 0.172$, Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}). SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy; context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which eliminates model-level randomness; bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":73,"oldLines":7,"newStart":73,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}"," \\label{sec:metrics}"," ","-\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.","+\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type."," "," "," \\subsection{Model and Inference}"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_015Ua68kRXPKGVFHvvh79cNH","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"2c09d51c-970d-4bcf-ac6b-72743c0cc73c","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:12:05.877Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex","oldString":"accuracy---exact match","newString":"accuracy (exact match","originalFile":"\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances uniformly across test instances; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 embeddings because its 66 fine-grained types include same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\\ ``person-actor'') that MiniLM's 384-d space does not separate well; a cross-embedding check confirms directional consistency (MiniLM $r = 0.172$, Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}). SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy; context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which eliminates model-level randomness; bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":73,"oldLines":7,"newStart":73,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}"," \\label{sec:metrics}"," ","-\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.","+\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type."," "," "," \\subsection{Model and Inference}"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01P17TaTU69vrDCQ8vFEcXkN","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"fd07cbd1-efb7-434c-b31d-65c79d688036","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:12:12.060Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex","oldString":"accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap","newString":"accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold). \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1, where any token overlap","originalFile":"\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances uniformly across test instances; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 embeddings because its 66 fine-grained types include same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\\ ``person-actor'') that MiniLM's 384-d space does not separate well; a cross-embedding check confirms directional consistency (MiniLM $r = 0.172$, Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}). SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on both NER and RE datasets (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). However, SCS is a partial proxy; context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which eliminates model-level randomness; bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":73,"oldLines":7,"newStart":73,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}"," \\label{sec:metrics}"," ","-\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.","+\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold). \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1, where any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type."," "," "," \\subsection{Model and Inference}"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-6","id":"msg_01RrYn7rm3J7A2TvtVp4ns8t","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01GSfjUqCxpkZJ6brBUZUzcy","name":"Edit","input":{"replace_all":false,"file_path":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex","old_string":"(document-level)---near or exceeding halving","new_string":"(document-level), near or exceeding halving"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":428,"cache_read_input_tokens":119246,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":428},"output_tokens":1,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"ce0fb614-e174-42bf-a3f1-b2cf20984efd"}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01GSfjUqCxpkZJ6brBUZUzcy","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"39b96d94-3cbe-4508-91e0-b6a82eb53235","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:12:15.509Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex","oldString":"(document-level)---near or exceeding halving","newString":"(document-level), near or exceeding halving","originalFile":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nThis study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), defined as pairwise embedding cosine similarity, and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates along two dimensions: count-associated recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-associated type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp), an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded association in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{First controlled validation that embedding similarity predicts type confusion in zero-shot IE.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas, invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across RE tasks and schema sizes.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$); applicability to span-level tasks (NER, ED) requires confound adjustment (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} NER merge strategies collapse precision; all practical RE routing strategies fall below the single-pass baseline despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp, because constrained decoding eliminates reliable confidence signals (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":1,"oldLines":7,"newStart":1,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\section{Introduction}"," \\label{sec:introduction}"," ","-Training-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.","+Training-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level), near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable."," "," What explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work})."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01MjRx5avugK9jZwyEbWeAmB","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/related_work.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"dfe82dff-a0ed-477b-888f-fc7cd23934b0","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:12:19.597Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/related_work.tex","oldString":"cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal","newString":"cocchieri-2025-zeroner}, both orthogonal","originalFile":"\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}\nTraining-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \\cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \\cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \\cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \\cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \\cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\nThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification (not partitioning quality) is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation factors. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\nOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}, explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":7,"oldLines":7,"newStart":7,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}"," The most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification (not partitioning quality) is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation factors. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability."," ","-\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.","+\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}, both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching."," "," \\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}
